·3· · · · · · ·MR. BUTLER:· Good morning, and welcome

·4· ·to the Office of the United States Trade

·5· ·Representative, in conjunction with the

·6· ·Interagency Section 301 Committee, is holding

·7· ·this public hearing in connection with the

·8· ·Section 301 investigation of Brazil's acts,

·9· ·policies, and practices related to digital trade

10· ·and electronic payment services, unfair

11· ·preferential tariffs, anti-corruption

12· ·enforcement, intellectual property protection,

13· ·ethanol market access, and illegal deforestation

14· ·initiated in accordance with specific direction

15· ·of the President on July 15th, 2025.

16· · · · · · ·On June 1s, 2026 the Trade

17· ·Representative determined that certain of

18· ·Brazil's acts, policies, and practices related

19· ·to physical trade, and electronic payment

20· ·services, unfair preferential tariffs, anti-

21· ·corruption enforcement, intellectual property

22· ·protection, ethanol market access, and illegal

·1· ·deforestation are unreasonable, are

·2· ·discriminatory, and burden or restrict U.S.

·3· ·Commerce and are therefore actionable under

·4· ·Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974.

·5· · · · · · ·Detailed information concerning this

·6· ·investigation is set out in Federal Register

·7· ·notices published on July 18th, 2025 at 90 Fed

·8· ·Reg 34069 and June 4th, 2026 at 91 Fed Reg

·9· ·33854.· The notices are, also, available on the

10· ·USTR webpage under the Section 301

11· ·investigations page.

13· ·receive public testimony regarding the proposed

14· ·action to be taken in the investigation

15· ·following the trade representatives

16· ·determination of actionability.· Consistent with

17· ·the rules and procedures set forth in the June

18· ·4th, 2026 Federal Register notice, witnesses

21· ·today's hearing.

10· · · · · · ·MR. BOLSONARO:· Mr. Chairman,

11· ·distinguished members of the Commission, ladies

12· ·and gentlemen, good morning everyone.· My name

13· ·is Flavio Bolsonaro, I'm a senator of the

14· ·Federative Republic of Brazil, Leader of the

15· ·Opposition, and a president pre-candidate for my

16· ·country's October election.· Last month, I met

17· ·with President Trump, Vice President Vance and

18· ·Secretary Rubio to urge the United States not to

19· ·impose tariffs on Brazilian companies.· Today I

20· ·would like to share with this Commission the

21· ·same arguments I presented to them and explain

22· ·why that request serves the interests of both


                         Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433             Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    12

·1· ·Brazil and the United States.

·2· · · · · · ·The 2025 data show that these tariffs

·3· ·have not produced the results the United States

·4· ·intended.· Instead, they have been politically

·5· ·exploited by Brazil's current administration.

·6· ·The very government these measures were meant to

·7· ·pressure has gained strength in the polls while

·8· ·Brazil's trade with China reached a record 171

·9· ·billion dollars, more than double its 83 billion

10· ·dollars in trade with the United States.· China

11· ·has absorbed Brazil exports that were displaced

12· ·from the American market by these tariffs.· In

13· ·other words, every additional tariff strengthens

14· ·the very government it is intended to pressure.

15· · · · · · ·Because of the little time available

16· ·today I will address only two issues.· All six

17· ·are discussed in detail in the written statement

18· ·I have submitted to this Commission.· The first

19· ·is censorship.· In the confidential orders

20· ·directed at American digital platforms did not

21· ·originate in Brazil's national congress.

22· · · · · · ·They were issued by a justice of


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    13

·1· ·Brazil's Federal Supreme Court, and by President

·2· ·Lula's administration based on executive decrees

·3· ·and judicial rulings rather than legislation

·4· ·duly enacted by Parliament.· A 25 percent tariff

·5· ·penalizes the entire Brazilian people except the

·6· ·very authorities responsible for those

·7· ·decisions.

·8· · · · · · ·The second issue is corruption.

·9· ·Corruption is one of the greatest scourges

10· ·facing the Brazilian people.· There is no

11· ·disagreement on that, but corruption has

12· ·identifiable perpetrators.· The four largest

13· ·corruption scandals in Brazil's recent history,

14· ·the Mensalao scheme, the case uncovered by

15· ·Operation Car Wash, the fraud involving Brazil's

16· ·National Social Security· institute, the INSS,

17· ·under which President Lula's own son is among

18· ·those under investigation, and the Banco Master

19· ·case all occurred under government led by the

20· ·Workers Party, the political party currently in

21· ·power.· Brazil, then, experienced four years

22· ·under President Bolsonaro without a single


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                     14

·1· ·proven corruption scandal.· Corruption has

·2· ·become a defining feature of Brazil's political

·3· ·left.· The Brazilian people should not be

·4· ·punished for it.

·5· · · · · · ·The third issue is Pix.· Pix, Brazil's

·6· ·instant payment system was created during the

·7· ·Bolsonaro administration.· Pix is not a problem

·8· ·to be fixed, it's a solution.· It expanded

·9· ·financial inclusion by bringing millions of

10· ·Brazilians, especially the poorest into the

11· ·formal economy.

12· · · · · · ·That progress, also, directly

13· ·benefitted American companies, as the volume of

14· ·transactions processed through payment cards

15· ·issued by U.S. brands continued to grow

16· ·alongside the widespread adoption of Pix, since

17· ·those companies provide service that complements

18· ·rather than competes with Brazil's instant

19· ·payment system.

20· · · · · · ·Brazil will hold presidential elections

21· ·in October.· Within just 90 days the country's

22· ·political landscape will look entirely

   (202) 234-4433            Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f

·1· ·different. Imposing tariffs now that would be

·2· ·difficult to reverse rewards those responsible

·3· ·for the actions in question while punishing

·4· ·those who have borne their consequence, would be

·5· ·the worst possible moment to act.                  I

·6· ·respectfully ask this Committee, do not impose

·7· ·tariffs on Brazil.

·8· · · · · · ·Preserve the success of Pix, cancel

   (202) 234-4433              Washington DC                www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f

·1· · · · · · ·When I last appeared before you, I

·2· ·explained that Brazil had not adopted acts,

·3· ·policies, practices that unjustifiably burden or

·4· ·restrict U.S. commerce.· On the contrary, our

·5· ·two economies are quite complementary.· CNI has

·6· ·since submitted detailed written comments in

·7· ·this phase and together the evidence in this

·8· ·record, and the progress achieved since the data

·9· ·on Brazil was collected does not support the

10· ·remedies USTR has proposed. And let me begin

11· ·there.· The proposed action is a uniform 25

12· ·percent tariff on almost all goods from Brazil.

13· · · · · · ·Section 301 requires that any action is

14· ·proportionate to the practice found, it is

15· ·feasible in light of the effect of the· U.S.

16· ·economy, and it is reasonably calculated to

17· ·eliminate those preferences.· A blanket tariff

18· ·on nearly all Brazilian goods with some

19· ·exceptions meets none of those requirements.

20· · · · · · ·It sweeps in products with no

21· ·connection to any practice under investigation

22· ·and at a scale that is highly disproportionate


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    17

·1· ·to any alleged impact.· Just as important,

·2· ·Brazil has made demonstrable progress since the

·3· ·data USTR relied on was collected.

·4· · · · · · ·On deforestation, Amazon alerts fell

·5· ·61.4 percent in May '26 compared to a year

·6· ·earlier.· This was the largest reduction for

·7· ·that month since monitoring began.· On

·8· ·intellectual property, patent payment pendency

·9· ·is now 2.9 years, comparable, therefore, to the

10· ·United States, not the seven year asserted at

11· ·initiation.

12· · · · · · ·On anti-corruption, active sanctions

13· ·against private entities rose from 68 in 2020 to

14· ·771 in 2024.· On issue after issue engagement

15· ·and Brazil's own enforcement are producing the

16· ·outcomes that the United States seeks.· Second,

17· ·I urge USTR to avoid further disruption, and

18· ·unnecessary harm, and to choose a negotiated

19· ·path instead.

20· · · · · · ·The premise of a rebalancing tariff is

21· ·simply absent here.· United States does not run

22· ·a deficit with Brazil, it runs one of its


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f

·1· ·largest surpluses, 41.8 billion in goods and

·2· ·services in 2025, concentrated in high value

·3· ·sectors where American workers excel, such as

·4· ·machinery, aircraft, and chemicals.

·5· · · · · · ·It is precisely this high-value

·6· ·American trade, much of which depends on

·7· ·Brazilian imports that this 25 percent tariff

·8· ·would put at risk, falling on American

·9· ·manufacturers, farmers, and consumers who cannot

10· ·re-source at comparable prices or volumes.

11· · · · · · ·The two governments have already

12· ·described their recent meetings as constructive.

13· · The responsible course is to resolve U.S.

14· ·concerns through the established bilateral

15· ·channels not to inflict lasting damage on a

16· ·mutually beneficial relationship.

·1· ·consumption.

12· ·outpacing Mexico and the Dominican Republic by

13· ·millions of units, though the overall market is

20· ·reliant on Asian leather footwear production.

22· ·tariffs on Brazilian leather footwear would


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f

·1· ·developed during this investigation does not

·3· ·justify additional tariffs.

·4· · · · · · ·So, allow me to briefly address just

·5· ·three issues.· The first one is tariffs.· Brazil

·6· ·is one of the very few major economies with

·7· ·which the U.S. enjoys a manufacturing trade

·8· ·surplus.· And that is difficult, of course, to

·9· ·reconcile with the proposition that Brazil

10· ·systematically discriminates against American

11· ·exports.

12· · · · · · ·One reason is quite straightforward:

13· ·the average tariffs effectively applied to

14· ·imports from the United States, 2.6 percent, is

15· ·far below Brazil's average bound tariff of

16· ·approximately 12.5 percent.

17· · · · · · ·The USTR suggests that this reflects

18· ·non-automatic import mechanisms, but

19· ·respectfully, that characterization is

20· ·incomplete.· These regimes, including the ex-

21· ·tariff regime and drawback, they have existed

22· ·for decades.· They are established by law, and


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f
                                                                    66

·1· ·they follow very transparent, predictable, and

·2· ·widely accessible procedures.

·3· · · · · · ·American companies have long been among

·4· ·their principal beneficiaries, receiving duty-

·5· ·free treatment for capital goods and

·6· ·intermediate inputs widely used by Brazilian

·7· ·industry.· The Mercosur-India Agreement

·8· ·illustrates this limited scope of these

·9· ·preferential tariffs.· It covers only about 20

10· ·percent of Brazil's imports from India, and only

11· ·13 tariff lines receive full duty-free

12· ·treatment.

13· · · · · · ·So let me now address environmental

14· ·protection, which is the second issue.· Illegal

15· ·deforestation is a crime in Brazil.· It is not

16· ·an industrial policy, and Brazilian companies

17· ·derive no benefit from it, quite the opposite.

18· · · · · · ·It damages the international reputation

19· ·of Brazilian industry and threatens one of the

20· ·country's greatest economic assets.· Brazil

21· ·faces enormous enforcement challenges in

22· ·protecting an area comparable to half the


                       Neal R. Gross and Co., Inc.
   (202) 234-4433           Washington DC            www.nealrgross.com  YVer1f

·1· ·continental U.S. and bordered by seven

·5· ·by 37.5 percent between August '25 and May '26,

·3· ·mouldings and millwork products.· Including

·4· ·these products in the action would not only

·5· ·mitigate the cost competitive advantage that

·6· ·foreign benefitting from forced labor inputs,

·7· ·but also reduce demand for products of illegal

·8· ·deforestation.

·9· · · · · · ·The inclusion of wood products, a

10· ·critical export industry for Brazil, in this

11· ·action is important to compel Brazil to act on

13· ·important issues raised in this investigation,

15· ·corruption enforcement, intellectual property

16· ·protection, and ethanol market access.
